```python
a.sort()
print(*a)
```

Wait, is this always valid?

And since we can perform as many swaps as we want, and we've shown that we can move elements to any position (through a sequence of swaps), we can achieve any permutation with the same even/odd counts.

Therefore, sorting the entire array gives us the lexicographically smallest possible arrangement.

The answer is indeed to sort the array.

```python
n = int(input())
```

What is lexicographically the smallest array you can obtain?

An array $x$ is lexicographically smaller than an array $y$ if there exists an index $i$ such that $x_i<y_i$, and $x_j=y_j$ for all $1 \le j < i$. Less formally, at the first index $i$ in which they differ, $x_i<y_i$

The first line contains an integer $n$ ($1 \le n \le 10^5$) — the number of elements in the array $a$.

The only line contains $n$ space-separated integers, the lexicographically smallest array you can obtain.


-----Examples-----
Input
3
4 1 7

Output
1 4 7

2
1 1

Output
1 1

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Check if there is at least one odd and one even number
has_odd = any(x % 2 == 1 for x in a)
has_even = any(x % 2 == 0 for x in a)

if has_odd and has_even:
    a.sort()
    print(*a)
else:
    print(*a)
```

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

has_odd = any(x % 2 == 1 for x in a)
has_even = any(x % 2 == 0 for x in a)

if has_odd and has_even:
    a.sort()
    print(*a)
else:
    print(*a)
```

- We can swap any odd with any even
- This means the set of positions is connected by swaps
- We can move any element to any position through a series of swaps (by first moving it to a "free" position, then repositioning)

For example, to move element at position i to position j:
1. If i = j, do nothing
2. If i ≠ j, find a position k where a[k] is of different parity from a[i]
3. Swap a[i] and a[k] 
4. Now a[i] is at position k
5. Continue until a[i] is at position j

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
```

Actually, after working through the examples and thinking about it, here's the key insight:

**The relative positions of odd and even numbers can be changed arbitrarily. This means we can rearrange the odd numbers among themselves and the even numbers among themselves in any order.**

2. Sort the odd numbers in ascending order
3. Sort the even numbers in ascending order
4. Interleave them in the positions in a way that gives the lexicographically smallest result

Actually, we need to assign the smallest numbers to the earliest positions. But we can only place an odd number in a position if there's a corresponding even number to swap with it.

Wait, no - we can achieve any arrangement where we have the same number of odd and even numbers in each "parity group". But we can move elements freely.

After more thought: since we can swap any odd with any even, and we can do this repeatedly, we can achieve any permutation of the entire array, except that we maintain the same parity distribution.

But that's not true - we can actually achieve more than that. 

Actually, let me consider the invariant: the total number of odd elements and even elements is preserved.

But more importantly, since we can swap any odd with any even, we can achieve any arrangement where the first k elements contain any subset of the original odd and even elements, as long as the count of odd elements in those positions matches the count of odd elements originally.

Wait, no - the parity of each element doesn't change, so if I have k odd elements in the original array, I must have k odd elements in the final array.

Therefore, in the final array, there are exactly the same number of odd and even elements as in the original.

So the problem reduces to: given that we have a fixed number of odd and even numbers, we want to arrange them in the lexicographically smallest way.

But we can rearrange the odd numbers among themselves arbitrarily and the even numbers among themselves arbitrarily, because we can use swaps to move them.

For instance, if I have odd numbers [1, 3] and even numbers [2, 4], I can achieve any arrangement of these four numbers, as long as I maintain the odd/even count.

Wait, is that true? Let me verify:

Start with [1, 3, 2, 4]
Can I get [1, 2, 3, 4]?
- Swap 3 (odd) with 2 (even): [1, 2, 3, 4] → yes! Because 3+2=5 is odd.

Can I get [2, 1, 4, 3]?
- Swap 1 with 2: [2, 1, 4, 3] → yes! 1+2=3 is odd.

Can I get [1, 4, 2, 3]?
- Swap 1 with 4? 1+4=5 is odd → [4, 3, 2, 1]
- Then swap 4 with 2? 4+2=6 is even → no
- Swap 4 with 3? 4+3=7 is odd → [3, 4, 2, 1]
- Then swap 3 with 2? 3+2=5 is odd → [2, 4, 3, 1]
- Hmm, not getting [1, 4, 2, 3]

Actually, I can't get [1, 4, 2, 3] directly. But I think I can achieve it through a longer sequence.

The key insight might be that we can achieve any permutation of the odd numbers in the odd positions and any permutation of the even numbers in the even positions, but not necessarily any mixed arrangement.

Actually, after more research and thought, I believe the answer is:

We can achieve any arrangement where the relative counts of odd and even numbers are preserved, and we can freely permute the odd numbers among the positions that originally had odd numbers, and the even numbers among the positions that originally had even numbers.

But that seems too restrictive.

Wait, actually, since we can swap any odd with any even, this means that any element can move to any position, as long as we have a path of swaps.

But each swap exchanges positions. 

Actually, the group of permutations achievable is the entire symmetric group, because we can generate any transposition of two elements of different parity, and from there we can generate any permutation.

But that can't be right because we can only swap odd-even pairs.

Actually, with swaps of odd-even pairs, we can generate any permutation of the entire set of elements, because we can use a sequence of swaps to bubble any element to any position.

For example, to move element x from position i to position j:
- If i < j, we can swap x with some even element to the right of i, then swap that even element with the next position, and so on.
- But we need a "bridge" element.

Actually, the key insight from the problem is simpler: **we can achieve any arrangement of the odd numbers among the even positions, and vice versa.**

But looking back at the examples:

Example 1: [4, 1, 7] → [1, 4, 7]
- We have odd: [1,7], even: [4]
- Final: odd at pos 0, even at pos 1, odd at pos 2
- So we have two odd numbers and one even number

In the final array [1, 4, 7], we have odd at position 0 and 2, even at position 1.

But originally we had odd at position 1 and 2, even at position 0.

So the placement of odd/even numbers has changed.

In fact, in the final array, we have odd at positions 0 and 2, even at position 1.

So we can change the positions of the odd/even groups.

But we can't change the total count.

After more thought, I believe the correct solution is:

We can freely rearrange all elements in any order, because any swap between an odd and even allows us to move any element to any position.

This is because we can:
1. Move an element to an adjacent position by swapping it with an element of different parity (if available)
2. Use this to bubble sort the array

Since we have both odd and even numbers (unless all are the same parity), we can move any element to any position.

But what if all numbers have the same parity? For example, [1,1] → [1,1]

In this case, we cannot swap any two elements because 1+1=2 is even.

So in this case, no swaps are possible, and the array remains unchanged.

Therefore, the algorithm is:
1. If all elements are of the same parity (all odd or all even), then we cannot perform any swaps, so answer is the original array sorted in ascending order.
2. Otherwise, we can perform swaps between any odd and even, which means we can achieve any permutation of the elements. Therefore, the lexicographically smallest array is the sorted array of all elements.

But wait, let me check with the first example:
- [4,1,7]: has both odd and even → so we can achieve any permutation → sorted would be [1,4,7] → matches expected output.

Second example:
- [1,1]: both odd → same parity → cannot swap → output should be [1,1] → matches.

Another example: [2,3,4,5] 
- has both even and odd → can achieve any permutation → sorted should be [2,3,4,5] → [2,3,4,5]

But can we really achieve any permutation?

Let me try to move 5 (odd) to position 0:
- Original: [2,3,4,5]
- Swap 5 with 2 (even): [5,3,4,2] 
- Swap 5 with 3 (odd)? 5+3=8 even → no
- Swap 5 with 4 (even)? 5+4=9 odd → yes → [4,3,5,2]
- Swap 5 with 2 (even)? 5+2=7 odd → yes → [4,3,2,5]
- Swap 5 with 4? 5+4=9 odd → [4,3,2,5] → no progress
- To get 5 to position 0, I need to swap it with 2 or 4 first.

From [2,3,4,5], swap 5 with 2: [5,3,4,2]
Now I have 5 at position 0.

Yes! So I can achieve it.

Another example: [3,5,7] (all odd) → cannot swap any → output should be sorted [3,5,7]

But the problem says "we can perform the operation as many times as we want" - but if we can't perform any operation (since any odd+odd=even), then we can't change anything.

Therefore, the solution is:

1. Check if all elements have the same parity (all odd or all even)
2. If yes, output the sorted version of the array
3. If no, output the sorted version of the array (since we can achieve any permutation)

Wait, but is it really that simple?

In the case where we have both odd and even, can we achieve any permutation?

The key insight is that we can generate any transposition between an odd and even element. With a set of such transpositions, we can generate any permutation of the entire set.

In group theory, the set of transpositions of elements of different parity generates the full symmetric group when there is at least one odd and one even element.

Therefore, yes, when there is at least one odd and one even element, we can achieve any permutation of the array.

Therefore, the lexicographically smallest array is simply the sorted array of all elements.

So the algorithm is:
- Read the array
- Sort the array
- Output the sorted array

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [4,1,7] → sorted: [1,4,7] → matches
Example 2: [1,1] → both odd, but sorted: [1,1] → matches

Another test: [3, 2] → can swap? 3+2=5 odd → yes → can swap to [2,3] → sorted [2,3] → which is lexicographically smaller than [3,2] → correct.

Another test: [5, 2, 3] → has both odd and even → can achieve any permutation → sorted: [2,3,5] → which is lexicographically smallest.

Therefore, the solution is simply to sort the array.

But why did I think about the parity groups earlier? Because initially I thought we might have constraints, but the mathematical fact is that with at least one odd and one even, we can generate any permutation.

So the final solution is just to sort the array.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
a.sort()
print(*a)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Write a function `sumTimesTables` which sums the result of the sums of the elements specified in `tables` multiplied by all the numbers in between `min` and `max` including themselves.

For example, for `sumTimesTables([2,5],1,3)` the result should be the same as
```
2*1 + 2*2 + 2*3 +
5*1 + 5*2 + 5*3
```
i.e. the table of two from 1 to 3 plus the table of five from 1 to 3

All the numbers are integers but you must take in account:

* `tables` could be empty.
* `min` could be negative.
* `max` could be really big.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to calculate the sum of products where each number in `tables` is multiplied by each number from `min` to `max` (inclusive)

2. For each element in `tables`, we calculate: element × min + element × (min+1) + ... + element × max
   This can be simplified as: element × (sum of all numbers from min to max)

3. Then we sum all these results together

4. Edge cases:
   - Empty `tables` list should return 0
   - Negative `min` values are allowed
   - `max` could be very large, so we need to be careful about efficiency

## Mathematical Analysis

For a single table number `t` and range from `min` to `max`:
- Sum = t × (min + (min+1) + (min+2) + ... + max)
- The sum of consecutive integers from min to max is: (max - min + 1) × (min + max) / 2

So for multiple table numbers, we can calculate:
- For each table number `t`: t × (max - min + 1) × (min + max) / 2
- Total sum = sum of all these individual sums

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Handle empty tables case - return 0
2. Calculate the sum of integers from min to max using the formula
3. For each table number, multiply it by this sum
4. Sum all these products

## Verification with Example

Example: `sumTimesTables([2,5],1,3)`

- Range from 1 to 3: sum = 1+2+3 = 6
- For 2: 2 × 6 = 12
- For 5: 5 × 6 = 30
- Total: 12 + 30 = 42

Let's verify manually:
- 2×1 + 2×2 + 2×3 = 2 + 4 + 6 = 12
- 5×1 + 5×2 + 5×3 = 5 + 10 + 15 = 30
- Total: 12 + 30 = 42 ✓

## Implementation

```python
def sumTimesTables(tables, min_val, max_val):
    # If tables is empty, return 0
    if not tables:
        return 0
    
    # Calculate sum of integers from min_val to max_val
    # Sum = (number of terms) * (first + last) / 2
    num_terms = max_val - min_val + 1
    sum_range = num_terms * (min_val + max_val) // 2
    
    # Sum the products of each table number with the range sum
    total = 0
    for table in tables:
        total += table * sum_range
    
    return total

# Read input from stdin
tables_input = list(map(int, input().split()))
min_val = int(input())
max_val = int(input())

# Solve and print result
result = sumTimesTables(tables_input, min_val, max_val)
print(result)
```

The solution is efficient with O(n) time complexity where n is the length of the tables list, and it handles negative numbers and large ranges correctly using the mathematical formula for sum of arithmetic sequences.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to sum the results of multiplying each element in `tables` by every integer from `min` to `max` (inclusive)
2. For each number in `tables`, we calculate: `number * min + number * (min+1) + ... + number * max`
3. This is equivalent to: `number * (sum of all integers from min to max)`
4. We need to handle edge cases: empty tables, negative min values, large max values

## Mathematical Insight

For a single number `n`, the sum from `min` to `max` is:
- If min ≤ max: `n * (sum of integers from min to max)`
- Sum of integers from min to max = `(max - min + 1) * (min + max) // 2`

This formula works even when min is negative.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Handle empty tables**: If tables is empty, return 0
2. **Calculate sum from min to max**: Use arithmetic series formula
3. **For each table value**: Multiply it by the sum of the range
4. **Sum all these products**

Let me trace through the example:
- `tables = [2,5]`, `min = 1`, `max = 3`
- Range sum from 1 to 3 = (3-1+1) * (1+3) // 2 = 3 * 4 // 2 = 6
- For 2: 2 * 6 = 12
- For 5: 5 * 6 = 30
- Total: 12 + 30 = 42

Let me verify with the given example:
2*1 + 2*2 + 2*3 = 2 + 4 + 6 = 12
5*1 + 5*2 + 5*3 = 5 + 10 + 15 = 30
Total: 12 + 30 = 42 ✓

## Edge Cases to Consider

- Empty tables: return 0
- min > max: the problem states "including themselves" and "between min and max", so we assume min ≤ max
- Negative min: the arithmetic series formula works correctly
- Large max values: the formula is O(1), so it's efficient

## Implementation

```python
def sumTimesTables(tables, min_val, max_val):
    if not tables:
        return 0
    
    # Calculate sum of integers from min_val to max_val
    # Using arithmetic series formula: n * (first + last) / 2
    # where n = max_val - min_val + 1
    n = max_val - min_val + 1
    range_sum = n * (min_val + max_val) // 2
    
    # Sum the products: for each table value, table_value * range_sum
    total = 0
    for table_val in tables:
        total += table_val * range_sum
    
    return total

# Read input
import sys
data = sys.stdin.read().strip().split()
if not data:
    print(0)
else:
    tables = list(map(int, data[0].split(',')))
    min_val = int(data[1])
    max_val = int(data[2])
    
    result = sumTimesTables(tables, min_val, max_val)
    print(result)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to calculate the sum of products for each number in `tables` multiplied by each number in the range `[min, max]` (inclusive)
2. For each element in `tables`, we multiply it by every number from `min` to `max` and sum all these products
3. We need to handle edge cases: empty `tables`, negative `min`, and potentially large `max`

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let's trace through the example:
- `tables = [2,5]`, `min = 1`, `max = 3`
- For table element 2: 2×1 + 2×2 + 2×3 = 2 + 4 + 6 = 12
- For table element 5: 5×1 + 5×2 + 5×3 = 5 + 10 + 15 = 30
- Total: 12 + 30 = 42

## Mathematical Optimization

Instead of iterating through each element in the tables and then through each number in the range, we can optimize:

For a single table element `t`, the sum of `t × i` for `i` from `min` to `max` is:
`t × (sum of i from min to max)`

The sum of integers from `min` to `max` is: `(max - min + 1) × (min + max) / 2`

So total sum = `sum(tables) × (sum of i from min to max)`

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, it's not just the sum of tables multiplied by the sum of range. For each table element `t`, we have `t × (sum of range)`.

So total sum = `sum(tables) × (sum of range from min to max)`

But let me verify with the example:
- Sum of tables = 2 + 5 = 7
- Sum of range from 1 to 3 = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- 7 × 6 = 42 ✓

This matches! The optimization works.

## Edge Cases

1. Empty tables: return 0
2. Negative min: formula still works with negative numbers
3. Large max: the mathematical formula avoids iterating through large ranges

## Implementation

```python
def sumTimesTables(tables, min_val, max_val):
    # Handle empty tables
    if not tables:
        return 0
    
    # Calculate sum of the range from min_val to max_val
    # Sum of arithmetic sequence: count * (first + last) / 2
    count = max_val - min_val + 1
    range_sum = count * (min_val + max_val) // 2
    
    # Sum of all table elements
    tables_sum = sum(tables)
    
    # Total result is sum of tables times sum of range
    return tables_sum * range_sum

# Read input
lines = []
try:
    while True:
        line = input().strip()
        if line:
            lines.append(line)
except EOFError:
    pass

# Parse input
if lines:
    tables_input = lines[0].strip()
    min_input = int(lines[1].strip())
    max_input = int(lines[2].strip())
    
    tables = list(map(int, tables_input.split(',')))
    result = sumTimesTables(tables, min_input, max_input)
    print(result)
```

Wait, I need to check the input format. The problem says "Read the inputs from stdin", but doesn't specify exactly how many lines. Looking at the example, it seems like:
- First line: comma-separated values for tables
- Second line: min value
- Third line: max value

Let me revise to handle this properly:

```python
    # Total result
```

1. We need to calculate the sum of products for each number in `tables` multiplied by each number from `min` to `max` (inclusive)
2. For each table value, we multiply it by every number in the range [min, max]
3. We sum all these products together
4. Special cases: empty tables, negative min values, potentially large max values

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let's trace through the example:
- `tables = [2,5]`, `min = 1`, `max = 3`
- For table value 2: 2*1 + 2*2 + 2*3 = 2 + 4 + 6 = 12
- For table value 5: 5*1 + 5*2 + 5*3 = 5 + 10 + 15 = 30
- Total: 12 + 30 = 42

## Mathematical Optimization

Instead of iterating through each number in the range for each table value, we can optimize:

For a single table value `t` and range [min, max]:
- Sum = t * (min + (min+1) + (min+2) + ... + max)
- Sum of arithmetic sequence from min to max = (number of terms) * (first term + last term) / 2
- Number of terms = max - min + 1
- Sum of terms = (max - min + 1) * (min + max) / 2
- Final sum for table value t = t * (max - min + 1) * (min + max) / 2

## Edge Cases

1. Empty tables: return 0
2. Negative min: the formula still works with negative numbers
3. Very large max: we need to be careful about integer overflow, but in Python integers can be arbitrarily large

## Implementation Strategy

1. Handle empty tables case
2. For each table value, calculate the sum of products with the range
3. Sum all individual results

```python
def sumTimesTables(tables, min_val, max_val):
    if not tables:
        return 0
    
    total_sum = 0
    n = max_val - min_val + 1  # number of terms in the range
    
    for table in tables:
        # Sum from min_val to max_val
        range_sum = n * (min_val + max_val) // 2
        # Multiply by table value
        total_sum += table * range_sum
    
    return total_sum

# Read input
tables_input = list(map(int, input().split()))
min_input = int(input())
max_input = int(input())

# Solve and print result
result = sumTimesTables(tables_input, min_input, max_input)
print(result)
```

Wait, I need to be more careful about the arithmetic. Let me verify the formula:

For range [min, max]:
- Sum = min + (min+1) + (min+2) + ... + max
- This is an arithmetic sequence with first term min, last term max, number of terms (max - min + 1)
- Sum = (number of terms) * (first term + last term) / 2
- Sum = (max - min + 1) * (min + max) / 2

This is correct. However, since we're dealing with integers, we need to ensure we get integer results. The product (max - min + 1) * (min + max) is always even because:
- If (max - min + 1) is even, then the product is clearly even
- If (max - min + 1) is odd, then min + max must be even (since max - min is even when the count is odd), so their sum is even

So the division by 2 will always give an integer result.

Final code:

```python
# Read input

min_input = int(input())
max_input = int(input())

# Solve

    print(0)
else:

    total_sum = 0
    for table in tables_input:
        range_sum = n * (min_input + max_input) // 2
        total_sum += table * range_sum
    print(total_sum)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

The function `sumTimesTables(tables, min, max)` needs to:
1. Take a list of numbers in `tables`
2. For each number in `tables`, multiply it by every integer from `min` to `max` (inclusive)
3. Sum all these products together
4. Handle edge cases: empty tables, negative min values, large max values

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let's trace through the example:
- `tables = [2,5]`, `min = 1`, `max = 3`
- For table 2: 2×1 + 2×2 + 2×3 = 2 + 4 + 6 = 12
- For table 5: 5×1 + 5×2 + 5×3 = 5 + 10 + 15 = 30
- Total: 12 + 30 = 42

## Mathematical Optimization

Instead of iterating through each number from `min` to `max` for every table item, I can use mathematical properties:

For a single table value `t`:
- Sum from `min` to `max` of `t × i` = `t × (sum from min to max of i)`
- Sum from `min` to `max` of `i` = `(max - min + 1) × (min + max) / 2`

So the total sum = `sum(tables) × (max - min + 1) × (min + max) / 2`

But wait, this would be incorrect because each table value needs to be multiplied by the sum of the range.

Actually, for each table value `t`:
- Contribution = `t × (sum of integers from min to max)`

So total sum = `sum(tables) × (sum of integers from min to max)`

And `sum of integers from min to max` = `(max - min + 1) × (min + max) / 2`

This only works if the range is consecutive integers.

## Edge Cases to Consider

1. Empty tables: return 0
2. Negative min: the formula still works with negative numbers
3. Large max: the formula avoids iterating through all values

## Verification with Example

- `tables = [2,5]`, `min = 1`, `max = 3`
- Sum of integers from 1 to 3 = 1+2+3 = 6
- Sum of tables = 2+5 = 7
- Total = 7 × 6 = 42 ✓

Let me verify with another simple case:
- `tables = [3]`, `min = 2`, `max = 4`
- Expected: 3×2 + 3×3 + 3×4 = 6 + 9 + 12 = 27
- Sum of integers from 2 to 4 = 2+3+4 = 9
- Sum of tables = 3
- Total = 3 × 9 = 27 ✓

## Implementation

```python
def sumTimesTables(tables, min_val, max_val):
    # Handle empty tables
    if not tables:
        return 0
    
    # Calculate sum of integers from min_val to max_val inclusive
    range_sum = (max_val - min_val + 1) * (min_val + max_val) // 2
    
    # Sum all table values
    table_sum = sum(tables)
    
    # Total result is sum of tables multiplied by sum of range
    return table_sum * range_sum

# Read input
line = input().strip()
tables_str = line.split('[')[1].split(']')[0]
min_val, max_val = map(int, input().split())

# Parse tables
tables = list(map(int, tables_str.split(',')))
print(sumTimesTables(tables, min_val, max_val))
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to compute the sum of products for each number in `tables` multiplied by each number from `min` to `max` (inclusive)
2. For each table number `t`, we calculate `t * min + t * (min+1) + ... + t * max`
3. This can be factored as `t * (min + (min+1) + ... + max)`
4. The sum of integers from `min` to `max` is `(max - min + 1) * (min + max) // 2`

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Handle edge cases
- If `tables` is empty, return 0
- If `min` > `max`, we need to handle this case (though the problem states min to max including themselves, so we assume min ≤ max)

### Step 2: Calculate the sum of integers from min to max
The formula for sum of consecutive integers from `a` to `b` is: `(b - a + 1) * (a + b) // 2`

### Step 3: For each table number, multiply it by the sum of integers from min to max
Then sum all these products.

### Step 4: Optimize the calculation
Instead of calculating the sum of products for each table number separately, we can factor it:
- Total sum = sum(tables) * (sum from min to max)

This is because each table number `t` contributes `t * (sum from min to max)` to the total.

## Example verification
For `sumTimesTables([2,5],1,3)`:
- Sum from 1 to 3 = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Total sum = (2 + 5) * 6 = 7 * 6 = 42
- Manual calculation: 2*1 + 2*2 + 2*3 + 5*1 + 5*2 + 5*3 = 2 + 4 + 6 + 5 + 10 + 15 = 42 ✓

## Implementation

```python
def sumTimesTables(tables, min_val, max_val):
    # Handle empty tables
    if not tables:
        return 0
    
    # Calculate sum of integers from min_val to max_val
    # Using formula: (max_val - min_val + 1) * (min_val + max_val) // 2
    count = max_val - min_val + 1
    sum_range = count * (min_val + max_val) // 2
    
    # Sum all table values and multiply by the range sum
    total_sum = sum(tables) * sum_range
    
    return total_sum

# Read input
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to sum the results of multiplying each element in `tables` by every number from `min` to `max` (inclusive)
2. For each number in `tables`, we calculate its multiplication table from `min` to `max`
3. We sum all these individual products

For example with `sumTimesTables([2,5],1,3)`:
- 2×1 + 2×2 + 2×3 = 2 + 4 + 6 = 12
- 5×1 + 5×2 + 5×3 = 5 + 10 + 15 = 30
- Total = 12 + 30 = 42

## Key Insights

1. For a single number `x` in tables, the sum from `min` to `max` is: `x * (sum from min to max)`
2. The sum from `min` to `max` can be calculated as: `(max - min + 1) * (min + max) / 2`
3. We can factor out the table elements: `sum(tables) * (sum from min to max)`
4. This works even when min is negative or max is very large

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Handle empty tables case: return 0
2. Calculate the sum of numbers from min to max using arithmetic series formula
3. Multiply this sum by the sum of all elements in tables
4. Return the result

Let me verify with the example:
- tables = [2,5], min = 1, max = 3
- Sum of numbers from 1 to 3 = (3-1+1) * (1+3) / 2 = 3 * 4 / 2 = 6
- Sum of tables = 2 + 5 = 7
- Result = 7 * 6 = 42 ✓

This matches the expected result!

## Implementation

```python
def sumTimesTables(tables, min_val, max_val):
    # Handle empty tables
    if not tables:
        return 0
    
    # Calculate sum of numbers from min_val to max_val using arithmetic series formula
    # Sum = n * (first + last) / 2 where n = count
    count = max_val - min_val + 1
    sum_range = count * (min_val + max_val) // 2
    
    # Sum all elements in tables
    sum_tables = sum(tables)
    
    # Return the product
    return sum_tables * sum_range
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to calculate the sum of products where each element in `tables` is multiplied by each number from `min` to `max` (inclusive)
2. For each table value `t`, we calculate `t * min + t * (min+1) + ... + t * max`
3. This can be simplified using the arithmetic series formula: `t * (sum of numbers from min to max)`
4. The sum of numbers from `min` to `max` is `(max - min + 1) * (min + max) / 2`

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Handle edge cases
- If `tables` is empty, return 0
- If `min > max`, we need to handle this case, but from the example it seems `min ≤ max`

### Step 2: Calculate the sum of numbers from min to max
The sum of integers from `min` to `max` inclusive is:
- Count = `max - min + 1`
- Sum = `count * (min + max) / 2`

### Step 3: For each table value, multiply by this sum
For each value `t` in `tables`, contribution is `t * (sum of numbers from min to max)`

### Step 4: Total sum is sum of all individual contributions

Let me verify with the example:
- `tables = [2,5]`, `min = 1`, `max = 3`
- Sum from 1 to 3 = 1+2+3 = 6
- For table 2: 2 * 6 = 12
- For table 5: 5 * 6 = 30
- Total = 12 + 30 = 42

Manual calculation:
- 2*1 + 2*2 + 2*3 = 2 + 4 + 6 = 12
- 5*1 + 5*2 + 5*3 = 5 + 10 + 15 = 30
- Total = 42 ✓

### Step 5: Implementation considerations
- We need to handle negative numbers correctly (arithmetic sequence formula still works)
- The range could be very large, so we must use the mathematical formula to avoid iterating through all numbers
- We must handle empty tables (return 0)

```python
def sumTimesTables(tables, min_val, max_val):
    # If tables is empty, return 0
    if not tables:
        return 0
    
    # Calculate sum of numbers from min_val to max_val inclusive
    count = max_val - min_val + 1
    if count <= 0:
        return 0
    
    # Sum of arithmetic sequence: count * (first + last) / 2
    numbers_sum = count * (min_val + max_val) // 2
    
    # Sum all table values multiplied by the sum of numbers
    total = 0
    for t in tables:
        total += t * numbers_sum
    
    return total

# Read input
import sys
input_data = sys.stdin.read().strip().split()
if not input_data:
    print(0)
else:
    tables = list(map(int, input_data[0].split(',')))
    min_val = int(input_data[1])
    max_val = int(input_data[2])
    
    result = sumTimesTables(tables, min_val, max_val)
```

```python
    # Using integer division since we're dealing with integers
    numbers_sum = count * (min_val + max_val) // 2
```

    if count <= 0:
        return 0
    
    # Sum of arithmetic sequence: count * (first + last) / 2
    # Using integer division since we're dealing with integers
    numbers_sum = count * (min_val + max_val) // 2
    
    # Sum all table values multiplied by the sum of numbers
    total = 0
    for t in tables:
        total += t * numbers_sum
    
    return total

# Read input from stdin
data = sys.stdin.read().strip().split()
if not data:
    print(0)
else:
    # First element is the tables string, rest are min and max
    tables_str = data[0]
    min_val = int(data[1])
    max_val = int(data[2])
    
    # Parse tables string
    tables = list(map(int, tables_str.split(',')))
    
    result = sumTimesTables(tables, min_val, max_val)
    print(result)
